# Design System: LSAT Academy

## Overview

**Creative North Star: "LSAT Academy"**

One institution with two registers, and the seam between them is deliberate.

**The Hall** is the door. Unauthenticated, dark, and material: near-black ground, warm marble text, a bronze that carries the light, and a serif that behaves like carved lettering. Its own source calls it a museum nocturne, carved marble filmed in darkness, read by modern instruments. Its job is to make a prospective student feel they are entering somewhere, not signing up for something. It is the only place in the product where atmosphere outranks throughput.

**The Workroom** is everything past sign-in. Light, neutral, and quiet, because a student sits inside it for an hour reading dense argument text and the interface must not compete with the passage. There is no warmth here and that is the decision, not an oversight: warm accents need a dark ground to read, and on the Workroom's white they collapse. The Hall's bronze measures 8.58:1 on its own night and 2.30:1 on the Workroom's page. The two registers do not share a palette because they cannot.

What they do share is restraint about meaning. In both, colour is a signal. Green, red and amber say something happened. Nothing is coloured to be warm, to look premium, or to fill a space. Depth comes from hairlines and background tone rather than shadow, and hierarchy comes from size, weight and position rather than hue.

Inside the Workroom, individual rooms may commit fully to their own world. The eight Activity Centre worlds, the scoped bootcamps and the inbox each carry a painted identity behind a wrapper class. That is sanctioned and it is what an academy is: neutral corridors, characterful rooms. The corridor never takes on a room's paint.

**Key Characteristics:**
- Two registers, one institution: a dark marble Hall at the door, a light neutral Workroom inside.
- Colour means something or it is absent.
- Flat surfaces, hairline separation, no drop shadows.
- Precise and quiet controls that stay out of the reader's way.
- Rooms may be worlds; corridors never are.

## Colors

Two grounds, one shared vocabulary of meaning.

### Primary

- **Workroom Ink** (`#121212`): the Workroom's near-black. It is the text colour, the fill of every primary button, and the colour of the focus ring. The Workroom has no accent, so ink carries emphasis by itself.
- **Hall Bronze** (`#c6a467`): the Hall's warm light. Hairline borders on the call to action, a sheen across it on hover, and the selection highlight. It exists only on the Hall's night ground and never crosses into the Workroom.

### Secondary

- **Hall Cyan** (`#8ad8e2`): the Hall's cool instrument note against the bronze, and the colour of its focus outlines. Rare by design; it marks precision where bronze marks warmth.
- **Teacher Amber** (`#c7841f`): the single accent of the teacher workspace, with a hue-locked ramp for hover (`#af7318`), text on tinted fills (`#895910`) and a light step (`#e0a648`). Deliberately theme-independent: the workspace is always the light format. It is the one accent in the product, and it belongs to the operator, not the student.

### Tertiary

Individual rooms carry their own palettes behind a wrapper class and are documented in their own surface briefs, not here. The wrappers in use are `.classroom-root`, `.mc-bootcamp`, `.ff-bootcamp`, `.rc-bootcamp`, `.topic-abstract`, `.cs-punk`, and the eight Activity Centre worlds.

`.classroom-root` was added to this list on 2026-09-08, after an audit found the classroom running on Outfit and a warm palette while this record still described it as neutral Workroom chrome. It is a sanctioned world, not drift: the warmth and the typeface are deliberate and they are what makes the classroom feel like a study rather than a dashboard. The record was wrong, not the code. Its brief is `.impeccable/surfaces/classroom.md`. Note that this is the one sanctioned world that is also a corridor, so the rule below about corridors never being worlds now has exactly one documented exception.

### Neutral

- **Workroom Page** (`#fcfcfc`) and **Card** (`#ffffff`): the page ground and the surface that sits on it. One step of separation, no more.
- **Workroom Raised** (`#f7f7f7`) and **Fill** (`#f2f2f2`): the two tonal steps available for layering and for resting fills.
- **Workroom Ink Muted** (`#737373`): secondary text. Measures 4.62:1 on the page, which clears the floor with almost nothing to spare. Do not lighten it.
- **Workroom Hairline** (`#e0e0e0`) and **Field** (`#e6e6e6`): the borders that do the work shadows used to.
- **Hall Marble** (`#eae5da`), with secondary (`#a6a093`) and muted (`#6e695e`) steps: warm off-white text on the night ground.

### State (semantic only, never decorative)

- **Correct** (`#10b77f`) and **Incorrect** (`#f43e5c`): a student's attempt was right or wrong. Held under their own names, separate from system status, so a future shift to one cannot silently reframe the other.
- **Warn** (`#f59f0a`): a bonus, a stretch, a non-blocking notice.
- **Success** (`#21c45d`), **Warning** (`#f59f0a`), **Destructive** (`#dc2828`): system status, not student performance.

### Named Rules

**The Ground Decides Rule.** A warm colour is legible on the Hall's night and illegible on the Workroom's page. Never carry a colour across the seam on the strength of liking it; measure it against the ground it will land on first.

**The Meaning-Only Rule.** A colour appears in the Workroom because something is right, wrong, cautionary, or dangerous. There is no fifth reason. If a surface feels flat and the impulse is to add a colour, the answer is type and space.

**The Named Ground Rule.** No colour is written as a literal hex in a component. It resolves from a token, and a token that resolves to nothing is a defect, not a subtle effect. Three colour names in this codebase pointed at variables that were never defined, and the product silently lost its focus ring for five months.

## Typography

**Workroom Body:** Lexend (with Outfit, then the system stack)
**Hall Display:** Fraunces (with Iowan Old Style, Times New Roman, serif)
**Hall Inscription:** Cinzel (with Trajan Pro, serif)
**Mono:** IBM Plex Mono
**Question Lock:** Outfit, held deliberately for LSAT question surfaces

**Character:** The Workroom is one humanist sans doing everything, sized and weighted rather than swapped. The Hall is a serif institution: an optical serif for its headings and an inscriptional face for its few carved lines. The split is the whole typographic idea. Inside, type gets out of the way; at the door, type is the architecture.

### Hierarchy

- **Title** (600, 24px, line-height 1, tracking -0.025em): card and section headings in the Workroom.
- **Body** (400, 14px rising to 16px at 640px and above, line-height 1.6): everything a student reads in the Workroom chrome.
- **Question** (400, 16px, line-height 1.6, Outfit): LSAT stimulus and answer choices. Font-locked to Outfit even though the rest of the app moved to Lexend, so question text does not shift when the shell's face changes.
- **Label** (500, 14px): button and control labels.
- **Hall Display** (380, fluid, tracking -0.015em, Fraunces): Hall headings. The unusually light 380 weight is the point; it reads as carved rather than printed.
- **Hall Inscription** (500, tracking 0.05em, Cinzel): the Hall's few short carved lines. Never body copy.
- **Hall Mono** (400, 13px, tracking 0.04em, IBM Plex Mono): the Hall's instrument labels.

### Named Rules

**The One Face Rule.** The Workroom uses a single family. Hierarchy is built from size, weight and space. A second family inside the Workroom is a defect unless it is the Outfit question lock.

**The Question Never Moves Rule.** LSAT question text is font-locked. A change to the shell's typeface must not reflow a passage a student is mid-way through reading.

**The Serif Stays At The Door Rule.** Fraunces and Cinzel belong to the Hall. A serif appearing in the Workroom chrome means the seam has leaked.

## Layout

The Workroom is a centred single column with generous side gutters. The container centres by default, carries 32px of horizontal padding, and stops widening at 1400px.

Breakpoints are the framework defaults up to 1280px, with the largest step pulled in to 1400px. Body type steps from 14px to 16px at 640px, and form controls are forced to 16px below 768px so that iOS does not zoom the page when a field takes focus. Named breakpoints `phone` (up to 600px), `tablet` (601 to 900px) and `desktop` (901px and up) exist for bootcamp surfaces.

Spacing runs on a 4px base. The steps in real use are 4, 8, 16, 24 and 32. Card padding is 24px on all sides; button padding is 8px vertical and 16px horizontal.

Reading measure matters more here than in most products. Passage and stimulus text is held to a comfortable line length rather than allowed to run the full container width.

### Named Rules

**The Reading Measure Rule.** Any surface carrying an LSAT passage, stimulus, or explanation constrains its own line length. The container's maximum is a ceiling for layout, never a licence for prose.

## Elevation & Depth

**Flat, with hairline separation.** Surfaces sit on the page rather than above it. A card is distinguished from its ground by a one-pixel border and, where more is needed, by one step of background tone. There are no drop shadows in the Workroom.

This is a decision, not a description. The codebase currently ships five shadow tokens tuned for a dark ground, from `0 1px 3px rgba(0,0,0,0.4)` up to `0 8px 32px rgba(0,0,0,0.7)`, and applies the smallest of them to every card. Because the app ships light by default, those shadows land at 40 percent black under a white card. The light theme never redefined them, and the one glow token is white, so it is invisible on white. The tokens are retired by this record and the code has not caught up yet.

The Hall is the exception and keeps its own depth: fixed ambience gradients, a grain overlay, and an inset highlight on its call to action. That depth is atmospheric rather than structural, and it does not transfer.

### Named Rules

**The Flat Rule.** Depth in the Workroom comes from a hairline or a tonal step. A `box-shadow` on a Workroom surface is a defect.

**The Two-Step Rule.** Page, then card, then at most one raised step. A surface that needs a fourth level of layering is a surface that needs restructuring.

## Shapes

Corners are gently rounded and consistent: 8px for cards and containers, 6px for buttons and inputs, 4px for the smallest chips and insets, and a full pill only for genuine badges. The scale derives from one 0.5rem radius token, so a change to the base moves the whole system together.

The Hall uses a near-square 2px radius instead. Its edges are cut, not softened, which is what separates a carved surface from a card.

Borders are one pixel. There is no second border weight in the Workroom.

### Named Rules

**The Hairline Rule.** One pixel, always. A thicker border used as a coloured accent stripe on the leading edge of a surface is banned, including when faked with an inset shadow. Active state is carried by background tone.

## Components

### Buttons

- **Character:** precise and quiet. A button is a plain rectangle with a label. It darkens slightly on hover and does nothing else. It does not lift, does not cast a shadow, and does not scale on press.
- **Shape:** gently rounded (6px).
- **Primary:** ink fill (`#121212`), page-coloured label, 8px by 16px padding.
- **Outline:** page background, one-pixel field border, ink label.
- **Ghost:** no fill at rest, a tonal fill on hover.
- **Sizes:** default 44px tall, small 40px, large 48px, icon 44 by 44. Every size clears the 44px touch minimum by being that height, never by relying on wrapped text or padding accidents.
- **Focus:** a solid two-pixel ink outline, offset two pixels from the edge. This is the only focus treatment in the Workroom.
- **Hall CTA:** 60px tall, near-square corners, transparent fill over a bronze hairline, with a bronze sheen that sweeps across on hover and a cyan outline on focus.

### Cards / Containers

- **Corner:** 8px.
- **Background:** card white on the page ground.
- **Border:** one-pixel hairline. This is what separates the card, not a shadow.
- **Shadow:** none.
- **Padding:** 24px, with header, content and footer sharing that inset.

### Inputs / Fields

- **Style:** page background, one-pixel field border, 6px corners, 44px minimum height, 8px by 12px padding.
- **Focus:** the same two-pixel ink outline as buttons, offset two pixels. Do not express focus as a border colour change alone; a border shift is too quiet to serve as the only indicator.
- **Mobile:** 16px text below 768px, so focusing a field never zooms the viewport.

### Navigation

Neutral chrome throughout. The sidebar runs one tonal step off the page ground with a hairline edge, and the active item is marked by background tone rather than by a coloured rule on its leading edge.

### Signature Component: the scoped world

The product's distinctive pattern is not a control, it is a container. A room commits fully to its own palette, typefaces and atmosphere behind a single wrapper class, and nothing inside it leaks out. Scene layers are `aria-hidden` furniture painted beneath the content, so the accessible tree is identical whether the room is painted or bare. A new world is added by writing a new wrapper and its prefixed tokens, never by adding hex to a shared component.

## Do's and Don'ts

### Do:

- **Do** resolve every colour from a token, and treat a token that resolves to nothing as a defect. Verify a new token renders before documenting it.
- **Do** give every interactive element a visible focus indicator: a solid two-pixel ink outline offset two pixels in the Workroom, cyan in the Hall.
- **Do** check contrast against the ground the colour will actually land on, in the theme that actually ships, in a browser. The theme file has read as correct while the page rendered a light card on a near-black ground.
- **Do** separate surfaces with a one-pixel hairline and one tonal step.
- **Do** keep colour semantic: correct, incorrect, warning, danger. Nothing else earns it.
- **Do** scope a new room's identity behind its own wrapper class with prefixed tokens, the way the bootcamps and the eight Activity worlds already do.
- **Do** hold LSAT passage and stimulus text to a readable line length, and leave it on the Outfit question lock.
- **Do** end every world's stylesheet with a `prefers-reduced-motion` block that stops continuous motion rather than speeding it up.

### Don't:

- **Don't** add a `box-shadow` to a Workroom surface. Depth is hairline and tone.
- **Don't** carry a Hall colour into the Workroom. Bronze reads 8.58:1 on the Hall's night and 2.30:1 on the Workroom's page.
- **Don't** introduce a warm or metallic accent into the Workroom to make it feel premium. Premium here is depth, hierarchy and type.
- **Don't** remove the browser's focus outline without shipping a replacement in the same rule. `outline-none` with no working ring is worse than leaving the default alone.
- **Don't** animate the focus indicator. A `transition-all` on a control means the ring fades in, which delays the one signal a keyboard user depends on.
- **Don't** use a border thicker than one pixel as a coloured accent stripe, including when faked with an inset shadow.
- **Don't** put a second typeface into the Workroom chrome, and don't let a serif cross in from the Hall.
- **Don't** animate layout properties. Animate `opacity`, `transform` and `color`. No bounce, no elastic, no lift on hover.
- **Don't** write a hex value into a component. If a room needs its own colour, promote it to named tokens behind that room's wrapper.

Tool result:
# Activity Centre

The home for bespoke, coach-led skill exercises (e.g. Negation Station) that a student is assigned and works through conversationally. Distinct from the question-bank MCQ **Drill** engine: Activities are authored content with their own answer keys, not timed runs over `qid` questions.

## Language

**Activity Centre**:
The module that hosts and lists Activities. The student's home for assigned skill work that isn't question-bank drilling.
_Avoid_: Activities Centre, Skills Centre, Drill Centre

**Activity**:
One bespoke skill exercise — a titled, self-contained set of authored items with an answer key, worked through with a coach (e.g. "Negation Station"). The unit a teacher assigns.
_Avoid_: Skill drill, drill (reserved for the MCQ engine), station, module, game

**Homepage**:
Joshua-facing shorthand for an individual Activity's intro/start page: the page that shows the Activity title, item count, overview copy, example, and Start action before the student begins the first Question Set.
_Avoid_: LSAT U landing page, Foyer, Activity Centre list

**Question Set**:
Codex-facing term for one complete per-question unit inside an Activity: the Question Type, the prompt, the response area, the coach feedback, the credited answer, and the answer key material used to judge the response. In the UI, this should still be labeled "Question N"; when Joshua says "question set," he means this full unit, not the collection of all questions.
_Avoid_: Question collection, drill set, item bank

**Question Type**:
The classification pill inside a Question Set that names what kind of statement or reasoning form the student is working with (e.g. "Universal Statement," "Conditional Statement," "Causal Statement"). This is the canonical term for the label currently shown above the prompt.
_Avoid_: Category, tag, badge, label

**Drill**:
Reserved term. The existing question-bank engine: a timed run over multiple-choice questions pulled by `qid`. An Activity is NOT a Drill.

**Item**:
One prompt within an Activity that the student responds to (e.g. a single statement to negate). Ordered easy→hard as authored.
_Avoid_: Question (overloaded with the qid question bank), problem, task

**Credited answer**:
The author-supplied correct response to an Item, used as ground truth when the coach judges a student's response. Some Items carry several acceptable phrasings.
_Avoid_: Solution, key answer, right answer

**Coach**:
The conversational AI tutor that presents Items one at a time, reads the student's free-text response, and gives immediate supportive feedback against the credited answer. Built on the existing AI-gateway tutor pattern (`analyze-stem-response`).
_Avoid_: Tutor (reserved for the standalone Tutor surface), bot, grader

**Verdict**:
The coach's judgement of a single student response: `correct`, `partial`, or `incorrect`. `partial` captures a near-miss the Activity exists to teach (e.g. a polar-opposite instead of a true negation). Drives the assignment score and future weakness analytics.
_Avoid_: Grade, mark, result

**Activity assignment**:
A per-student issued copy of an Activity, snapshotting its content at assign time and tracking the student's progress/score. Mirrors the homework `assignment` pattern; immutable snapshot once issued.
_Avoid_: Homework (that's the MCQ-set pattern), task

### Read, Recall, Colour

The four-step vocabulary of the RRC runner, plus the language its grading uses.
The source method's own words for these steps are listed under `_Avoid_` and are
never used in the product.

**Chunk**:
An authored cut of a stimulus, roughly a sentence, carrying the Details that fall inside it. A guide for her eye and the unit content is written in, never a boundary she is held to: she reads, recalls and colours in whatever units she likes.
_Avoid_: Line, segment, piece, part

**Read** (step 1):
The silent reading of a chunk. Nothing is spoken.
_Avoid_: Skim, study

**Retelling** (step 2):
Saying a chunk back out loud in her own words. **Retelling** is the word we use to each other and in code; the label a student reads is always **Tell it back**.
_Avoid_: Reading Recall (its former name, which put "Recall" on two different steps), Say it back (the old student label), translate, translation, paraphrase, summary, repeat-back

**Colouring In** (step 3):
Her spoken reaction to the chunk she just recalled — a thought, doubt, example, or joke that fits that chunk and no other.
_Avoid_: Quip, comment, annotation, reaction

**The Recall** (step 4):
The end of a stimulus, worked with the text gone: she says the whole argument back from memory.
_Avoid_: The Read (its former name, which collided with step 1), Retelling (that is step 2), CLIR, recap, rebound, takeaway

**Detail**:
One of the small authored facts a stimulus contains. PT102.S3.Q1 holds 21.
_Avoid_: Point, fact, bullet, item (reserved for an Activity's prompt)

**Coverage**:
How many of a stimulus's Details she carried, counted across everything she said about it rather than against any one Chunk.
_Avoid_: Accuracy, completeness, recall rate

**Reciting**:
Saying a stimulus back close to word-for-word. A failure of the Retelling, not a step of its own: Coverage looks perfect and nothing was understood.
_Avoid_: Parroting, copying, verbatim recall, memorising

**Listing**:
Delivering a stimulus as a flat run of facts rather than a story in her own words. The opposite failure to Reciting: Coverage looks perfect and the argument was never held together.
_Avoid_: Shopping list, itemising, enumerating

**Report Card**:
What a finished section produces: every stimulus graded on its own merits, showing what she missed and what she got perfect, with her own recording playable beside it. One player per stimulus, never per question, because two questions can share a stimulus and she recorded it once.
_Avoid_: Scorecard (informally taken by the MCQ Drill results screen), Verdict (that is the coach judging live, which this surface never does), results, grade, review

**Clip**:
One stimulus's worth of her recording, playable on the Report Card. A Clip is cut out of the single unbroken section recording after she finishes; it is never recorded as its own file, because the drill forbids stopping the microphone mid-section.
_Avoid_: Take, snippet, segment (that is the timing record the Clip is cut from), track

**Attempt**:
One complete run of one section by one student. A section may be attempted any number of times, and every Attempt keeps its own date, its own recording and its own Report Card. Nothing is overwritten and nothing is hidden once done.
_Avoid_: Try, retry, session (reserved for a tutoring session), run

**Ungraded**:
A stimulus the judge never answered for, because the call to it failed. Distinct from a stimulus she said nothing about, which is a real zero: this one has no measurement at all. It is excluded from Coverage and from every count of her habits, because reporting our outage as her behaviour is worse than reporting nothing.
_Avoid_: Failed, missing, unscored, zero, error

**Sweep**:
The scheduled re-drive of a finished section that never got a grade, or got a partial one. The client asks for grading the moment she stops and does not wait on the answer; the Sweep is what makes that ask safe to lose. A closed tab never costs a Report Card.
_Avoid_: Retry, cron, job, backfill, reconcile

### The section launch surface

The page a student meets before a section run, and the parts of it that carry
names. Nothing here is a phase of the runner; these are things drawn on one
page.

**Section Launch**:
The page a student lands on before running an RRC section: the method as four Beats, her last few Attempts, and the one control that starts the run. `SectionLaunch` in code, `.sl-` in CSS. It replaces the editorial intro for section mode only; the eight typed Activities keep theirs.
_Avoid_: The Chamber (the approved mockup's name for it, and already a world under `worlds/chamber/`), Homepage (that is an Activity's own intro page), intro, splash, lobby, dashboard

**Beat**:
Internal code term for one of the four method steps shown on Section Launch. It may remain in implementation names, but it is never student-facing. Students see **step**: Read it, Tell it back, Colour it, Recall it.
_Avoid in student-facing copy_: Beat, beats, phase, tab, card

**Tick**:
One mark in the row that draws a past Attempt: one per stimulus slot, placed at its authored `item_order` rather than its array position. Four states, because three would lie — `recorded`, `notJudged` (the judge never answered, so the mark is hollow), `silent` (she spoke nothing into that stimulus), `missing`.
_Avoid_: Dot, pip, square, cell, bar

**Target**:
The clock the section was meant to fit, taken from the assignment's time goal at her resolved accommodation. It measures elapsed time and nothing else, and a run is only ever compared against a Target equal to its own.
_Avoid_: Goal (`within_goal` in the database also carries method conditions, which Target deliberately excludes), limit, par, deadline, pace

import type { SectionPhase, SectionStimulus } from "./section";
import type { CoachNote } from "./coach";

/**
 * Is this page running inside another page's frame?
 *
 * A preview frame is never handed the microphone: the embed carries no
 * microphone permission, so `getUserMedia` is refused before the student is
 * ever asked. That looks identical to a student saying no, and "allow the
 * microphone for this site" is advice she cannot act on. So when the drill is
 * framed, the refusal names the real cause and offers the one thing that
 * works: the same address in a tab of its own.
 *
 * A cross-origin frame throws on the read, and a throw means framed.
 */
function isEmbedded(): boolean {
  try {
    return window.self !== window.top;
  } catch {
    return true;
  }
}


/**
 * One screen of a Read, Recall, Colour SECTION run.
 *
 * Presentational, like `RecallStage`, and separate from it because the rule it
 * keeps is different. v1 hides one chunk during its recall; a section has two
 * looks and nothing between them: the whole stimulus while she works it, and
 * no stimulus at all during The Recall
 * (`docs/adr/0013-she-chunks-freely-and-the-app-does-not-track-it.md`).

 * **It wears the real section's clothes.** The drill she is training for is sat

 * field, white header rail, one centred white paper card, the stimulus in
 * Fraunces at 19px. This screen replicates that contract exactly and removes
 * the answering half -- no question column, no choices, no verdict, no
 * notepad -- so the drill feels like the thing it is preparing her for rather
 * than like a separate app. The frame itself is not imported because it carries

/* Read, Recall, Colour, section mode.
 *
 * The v1 runner sits on a dark field because it shows one chunk at a time and
 * nothing during recall: there is barely anything on screen to light. A section
 * is the opposite surface. The whole stimulus is present for the whole
 * stimulus, it is the only thing on screen, and she is reading it closely for
 * twenty minutes. That is a document, and a document belongs on paper.
 *
 * So this screen wears the real section's clothes. `OfficialDrillFrame` already
 * mirrors LawHub -- indigo field, white header rail, one centred white card,
 * Fraunces at 19px -- and that is the surface she will sit on test day. Every
 * value below is taken from it rather than invented, and the answering half is
 * removed rather than restyled. No new dialect: this is the drill room.
 *
 * Everything after the frame exists to keep the text in exactly the same place
 * while its state changes.
 */

.ac-section {
  --sec-field: #1a1635;
  --sec-paper: #ffffff;
  --sec-ink: #111827;
  --sec-rule: #cbd5e1;
  --sec-chrome: #334155;
  --sec-warn: #b45309;
  --sec-sans: "Helvetica Neue", Helvetica, Arial, sans-serif;

  display: flex;
  min-height: 100vh;
  flex-direction: column;
  background: var(--sec-field);
  font-family: var(--sec-sans);
}

/* ---- the white rail ---------------------------------------------------- */

/* One column per child, and the count has to be kept: declared short once, the
   exit wrapped onto a second row and the rail grew the header from 46px to 96px
   on the drill's only screen. Six now -- position, rail, clock, the recording
   mark, Pause, the way out. */
.ac-section-head {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: auto 1fr auto auto auto auto;
  align-items: center;
  gap: 1.25rem;
  padding: 0.75rem 1.5rem;
  border-bottom: 1px solid #e2e8f0;
  background: var(--sec-paper);
  color: var(--sec-chrome);
  font-size: 0.8125rem;
}

/* Where she is in the section. The clock sits beside it; see
   `.ac-section-clock` for why it counts up rather than down. */
.ac-section-where {
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
}

.ac-section-where b {
  font-weight: 600;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
}

.ac-section-rail {
  height: 2px;
  overflow: hidden;
  border-radius: 9999px;
  background: #e2e8f0;
}

.ac-section-rail-fill {
  display: block;
  height: 100%;
  transform-origin: left center;
  background: var(--sec-chrome);
  transition: transform 0.4s cubic-bezier(0.16, 1, 0.3, 1);
}

/* Assignment-gated and full screen. Without a way out the only exit is the
   browser's back button, which is a trap rather than a design. */
.ac-section-exit {
  border: none;
  border-radius: 9999px;
  background: none;
  padding: 0.25rem 0.75rem;
  color: inherit;
  font: inherit;
  cursor: pointer;
  transition: background-color 0.15s ease, color 0.15s ease;
}

.ac-section-exit:hover {
  background: #f1f5f9;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
}

/* Stepping away, which is the thing that was missing.
 *
 * Same weight as Leave and next to it, because they answer the same question
 * and she should meet them together -- the whole failure this fixes is a
 * student who wanted this one and could only find that one. Deliberately far
 * from the paper: pausing is chrome, not a step in the drill, and nothing that
 * stops the clock may sit where a hand aiming for Space could land on it. */
.ac-section-pause {
  border: 1px solid var(--sec-rule);
  border-radius: 9999px;
  background: none;
  padding: 0.25rem 0.75rem;
  color: inherit;
  font: inherit;
  cursor: pointer;
  transition: background-color 0.15s ease, color 0.15s ease;
}

.ac-section-pause:hover {
  background: #f1f5f9;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
}

/* ---- the paper --------------------------------------------------------- */

.ac-section-field {
  display: flex;
  flex: 1;
  padding: 1rem;
}

/* The floor is a floor, not a promise the viewport can keep.
 *
 * `min-height: 700px` was written for a desktop and is taller than a 13-inch
 * laptop has room for. At a 664px viewport the card ran to 778px and pushed the
 * footer -- the instruction, the presence bar and the microphone error, which
 * are the only words on the screen telling her what to do and whether she is
 * being heard -- 55px below the fold. She would never see "Nothing is reaching
 * the microphone" on that machine.
 *
 * The `max-width: 640px` rule below did zero the floor, but it is a WIDTH
 * query, and the laptop that fails this is 1440 wide. Height is what was short,
 * so height is what the cap is written against.
 *
 * `min()` rather than a smaller constant, so the same bug cannot return at
 * 600px: the card asks for 700 and takes whatever the viewport can actually
 * spare, less the header and the field's own padding. On anything taller than
 * about 800px nothing changes at all. */
.ac-section-paper {
  display: flex;
  width: 100%;
  max-width: 1400px;
  min-height: min(700px, calc(100vh - 7rem));
  margin: 0 auto;
  flex-direction: column;
  overflow: hidden;
  border: 1px solid #cbd5e1;
  border-radius: 0.375rem;
  background: var(--sec-paper);
  box-shadow: 0 4px 18px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.35);
}

/* The stimulus is the only thing in the card, so its column is centred
   horizontally -- but it hangs from the top, exactly as it does in the column it
   used to share with a question.
 *
 * Centring it vertically was the first cut and it was wrong: stimuli run from
 * two lines to nine, so a centred block starts at a different height on every
 * one of the 23 and the whole page appears to jump between them. The spec bans
 * reflow inside a stimulus; this is the same failure one level up. */
.ac-section-body {
  display: flex;
  flex: 1;
  /* A flex item will not shrink below its content unless it is told it may. The
     stimulus is the part that can be scrolled past; the footer is not, so the
     reading area is what gives way when the card is short. */
  min-height: 0;
  align-items: flex-start;
  justify-content: center;
  overflow-y: auto;
  padding: 4rem 1.5rem 3rem;
}

.ac-section-stimulus {
  max-width: 52ch;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
  font-family: Fraunces, ui-serif, Georgia, serif;
  font-size: 19px;
  font-weight: 400;
  line-height: 1.7;
  /* Fraunces is a variable font with an optical-size axis. Left on `auto` the
     browser picks the cut from the rendered size; pin it to the text-optimised
     end so the hairlines stay put. Grayscale smoothing thins them too. */
  font-optical-sizing: none;
  font-variation-settings: "opsz" 14;
  -webkit-font-smoothing: subpixel-antialiased;
}

.ac-section-stimulus ::selection {
  background: #dbeafe;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
}

/* One inline run per chunk. Inline rather than block so a chunk that is part of
   a sentence still reads as that sentence: the cuts guide her eye, and nothing
   more. There are no per-chunk states any more -- no current, done or upcoming
   marker -- because the app does not track which chunk she is on
   (docs/adr/0013-she-chunks-freely-and-the-app-does-not-track-it.md). */
.ac-section-chunk {
  color: var(--sec-ink);
}

/* ---- the card's footer ------------------------------------------------- */

/* Pinned inside the card, and never the thing that gives way. Whatever else the
   card has to do with the height it is given, these words stay on screen. */
.ac-section-foot {
  display: flex;
  flex-shrink: 0;
  align-items: center;
  justify-content: space-between;
  gap: 1rem;
  padding: 0.875rem 1.5rem;
  border-top: 1px solid #e2e8f0;
  background: #f8fafc;
  font-size: 0.8125rem;
}

.ac-section-instruction {
  margin: 0;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
  font-weight: 500;
}

/* The footer's right-hand slot holds the fault and the key hint together, so
   neither has to take the other's place. Wraps rather than crushes, because the
   microphone warning is a whole sentence and the card is not always wide. */
.ac-section-foot-right {
  display: flex;
  align-items: center;
  justify-content: flex-end;
  gap: 1rem;
  flex-wrap: wrap;
}

.ac-section-hint {
  margin: 0;
  color: #64748b;
}

.ac-section-hint kbd {
  border: 1px solid var(--sec-rule);
  border-bottom-width: 2px;
  border-radius: 0.25rem;
  background: var(--sec-paper);
  padding: 0.125rem 0.5rem;
  font-family: inherit;
  font-size: 0.75rem;
  color: var(--sec-chrome);
}

/* Silence and a dead microphone look identical from her side, so a fault has to
   say so rather than let her talk into nothing for twenty minutes. */
.ac-section-error {
  margin: 0;
  color: var(--sec-warn);
  font-weight: 500;
}

/* The focus ring is graphite everywhere in the drill room, never an accent. */
.ac-section :focus-visible {
  outline: 3px solid rgb(29 29 31 / 0.35);
  outline-offset: 2px;
}

@media (max-width: 640px) {
  .ac-section-head {
    gap: 0.75rem;
    padding: 0.75rem 0.9rem;
  }

  .ac-section-pause,
  .ac-section-exit {
    padding: 0.25rem 0.5rem;
  }

  .ac-section-paper {
    min-height: 0;
  }

  .ac-section-body {
    padding: 2rem 1.25rem;
  }
}

/* A 13-inch laptop, once the browser has taken its chrome. The reading area's
   4rem of head room is the first thing worth spending when the card is this
   short, and spending it here keeps the stimulus off the footer. */
@media (max-height: 800px) {
  .ac-section-body {
    padding: 2rem 1.5rem 1.5rem;
  }
}

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  .ac-section-chunk,
  .ac-section-rail-fill,
  .ac-section-exit {
    transition: none;
  }
}

/* Elapsed time, in the rail beside the position line.
 *
 * Counting up, never down. The drill is timed and she has to be able to pace a
 * 25-minute section, but a number falling towards zero in the corner of her eye
 * produces exactly the rushed reading this drill treats. The goal sits after it
 * in lighter type so the elapsed figure is the one that reads first.
 */
.ac-section-clock {
  font-family: var(--sec-sans);
  font-size: 13px;
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
  color: var(--sec-chrome);
  white-space: nowrap;
}

.ac-section-clock i {
  font-style: normal;
  opacity: 0.55;
}

/* "Recording", in the rail, for the whole run.
 *
 * The one thing she asked to be able to see mid-section: that it is on. A
 * word and a dot, in the chrome rather than on the paper, so it is always in
 * the corner of her eye and never near the text. The word carries the meaning;
 * the red is on top of it. */
.ac-section-rec {
  display: inline-flex;
  align-items: center;
  gap: 0.4rem;
  font-family: var(--sec-sans);
  font-size: 0.75rem;
  letter-spacing: 0.08em;
  text-transform: uppercase;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
  white-space: nowrap;
}
.ac-section-rec--off {
  width: 0;
}

/* Paused, in the slot the recording mark had, and in the colour the rest of
   this screen uses for "something is not running". It does not pulse: the dot
   pulses to say the file is being written to, and during a break it is not. */
.ac-section-rec--paused {
  color: var(--sec-warn);
}

.ac-section-rec--paused i {
  background: var(--sec-warn);
  animation: none;
}
.ac-section-rec i {
  width: 0.5rem;
  height: 0.5rem;
  border-radius: 50%;
  background: oklch(0.58 0.2 27);
  animation: ac-section-rec-pulse 1.6s ease-in-out infinite;
}
@keyframes ac-section-rec-pulse {
  0%,
  100% {
    opacity: 1;
  }
  50% {
    opacity: 0.35;
  }
}
@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  .ac-section-rec i {
    animation: none;
  }
}

/* Presence, not a level meter.
 *
 * The one moving thing on a screen she talks at for twenty-one minutes. It
 * answers "is anything reaching it" and deliberately not "how am I doing":
 * no target, no colour change, no number, because nothing in this room is
 * allowed to read as a verdict before the section ends. Graphite, low
 * contrast, and beside the instruction rather than anywhere near the text.
 */
.ac-section-level {
  display: inline-block;
  width: 34px;
  height: 3px;
  margin-left: 0.625rem;
  overflow: hidden;
  border-radius: 9999px;
  background: #e2e8f0;
  vertical-align: middle;
}

.ac-section-level-fill {
  display: block;
  height: 100%;
  transform: scaleX(0);
  transform-origin: left center;
  background: var(--sec-chrome);
}

/* ---- leaving ------------------------------------------------------------ */

/* A half-finished section writes nothing by design, so the button that ends one
   is the most expensive control in the product. It asks first, over the run
   rather than instead of it: the stimulus stays behind the scrim, because she
   may well be here by accident and the way back is to see where she was. */
.ac-section-confirm {
  position: fixed;
  z-index: 50;
  display: flex;
  align-items: center;
  justify-content: center;
  padding: 1.5rem;
  background: rgb(10 8 24 / 0.62);
  inset: 0;
}

.ac-section-confirm-card {
  width: 100%;
  max-width: 26rem;
  border-radius: 0.5rem;
  background: var(--sec-paper);
  padding: 1.5rem;
  box-shadow: 0 18px 48px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.45);
}

.ac-section-confirm-title {
  margin: 0;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
  font-size: 1rem;
  font-weight: 600;
}

.ac-section-confirm-body {
  margin: 0.5rem 0 0;
  color: #475569;
  font-size: 0.8125rem;
  line-height: 1.6;
}

.ac-section-confirm-row {
  display: flex;
  justify-content: flex-end;
  gap: 0.5rem;
  margin-top: 1.25rem;
}

.ac-section-confirm-stay,
.ac-section-confirm-go {
  border-radius: 0.375rem;
  padding: 0.4375rem 0.875rem;
  font: inherit;
  font-size: 0.8125rem;
  cursor: pointer;
}

/* Staying is the default and looks like it. */
.ac-section-confirm-stay {
  border: 1px solid transparent;
  background: var(--sec-ink);
  color: var(--sec-paper);
  font-weight: 500;
}

.ac-section-confirm-go {
  border: 1px solid var(--sec-rule);
  background: none;
  color: #475569;
}

.ac-section-confirm-go:hover {
  border-color: #cbd5e1;
  color: var(--sec-warn);
}

/* ---- the ready beat ----------------------------------------------------- */

/* Three seconds between pressing Start and the first stimulus.
 *
 * The microphone opens on mount and the clock starts with it, so without this
 * the first thing a 25-minute recorded take captures is a student still
 * settling. It also puts the one instruction she needs -- Space, twice per
 * stimulus -- on screen at the moment it becomes true. */
.ac-section-ready {
  display: flex;
  flex: 1;
  flex-direction: column;
  align-items: center;
  justify-content: center;
  gap: 1.25rem;
  padding: 3rem 1.5rem;
  text-align: center;
}

.ac-section-ready-title {
  margin: 0;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
  font-family: Fraunces, ui-serif, Georgia, serif;
  font-size: 26px;
  font-weight: 400;
  font-optical-sizing: none;
  font-variation-settings: "opsz" 14;
}

.ac-section-ready-body {
  max-width: 34ch;
  margin: 0;
  color: #64748b;
  font-size: 0.875rem;
  line-height: 1.65;
}

.ac-section-ready-count {
  color: #94a3b8;
  font-size: 0.8125rem;
  font-variant-numeric: tabular-nums;
}

/* A break wears the ready beat's clothes on purpose. It is the same kind of
   moment -- the section open, the clock not running, one thing to press -- and
   a second full-card layout for it would be a second thing to keep true. */
.ac-section-ready--paused .ac-section-ready-title {
  color: var(--sec-warn);
}

/* The refused microphone reads as a fault rather than as a beat, so the heading
   takes the warning colour the footer uses for the same class of thing. */
.ac-section-ready[data-mic="refused"] .ac-section-ready-title {
  color: var(--sec-warn);
}

/* Two ways forward, neither of which existed. Asking again leads, because it is
   the one that keeps the section. */
.ac-section-ready-actions {
  display: flex;
  align-items: center;
  gap: 0.75rem;
  flex-wrap: wrap;
  justify-content: center;
}

.ac-section-ready-retry,
.ac-section-ready-leave {
  border-radius: 9999px;
  padding: 0.4rem 1.1rem;
  font: inherit;
  font-size: 0.8125rem;
  cursor: pointer;
  transition: background-color 0.15s ease, color 0.15s ease, border-color 0.15s ease;
}

.ac-section-ready-retry {
  border: 1px solid var(--sec-ink);
  background: var(--sec-ink);
  color: var(--sec-paper);
}

.ac-section-ready-retry:hover {
  background: var(--sec-chrome);
  border-color: var(--sec-chrome);
}

.ac-section-ready-leave {
  border: 1px solid var(--sec-rule);
  background: none;
  color: var(--sec-chrome);
}

.ac-section-ready-leave:hover {
  background: #f1f5f9;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
}

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  .ac-section-level-fill {
    transition: none;
  }
}


/* ---- leaving the drill room -------------------------------------------- */

/* The one composed moment in the drill.
 *
 * The runner is a cold indigo mirror of the real section; the Report Card is
 * warm paper. The jump between them was abrupt because there was nothing
 * between them: the field cut to the card in one frame, at the end of
 * twenty-five unbroken minutes, which is the moment she is most likely to
 * remember and the moment that felt most like a page reload.
 *
 * So the field lifts rather than cuts. The paper card holds its position and
 * fades with it, the indigo warms to the ground the Report Card sits on, and
 * the handover happens underneath. Nothing waits on it: the save, the upload
 * and the grading request all run through these nine hundred milliseconds, and
 * the transition is over long before any of them are.
 */
.ac-section.is-finishing {
  /* The Activity Centre's own canvas, by value rather than by eye.
     `--background` is scoped to `.activity-centre`, which this element is not
     inside, and landing two shades off it turns a handover into a visible step
     at the exact moment it is meant to be invisible. Kept beside the token it
     copies so a change to one is a change to look for in the other. */
  background: hsl(38 36% 93%);
  transition: background 900ms cubic-bezier(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1);
}

.ac-section.is-finishing .ac-section-head {
  opacity: 0;
  transition: opacity 500ms ease;
}

.ac-section.is-finishing .ac-section-paper {
  border-color: transparent;
  box-shadow: 0 1px 3px rgb(0 0 0 / 0.04);
  transition:
    box-shadow 900ms cubic-bezier(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1),
    border-color 900ms ease;
}

/* What she reads while it happens. It arrives after the field has started to
   warm, so the sentence appears on paper rather than on the drill room. */
.ac-section-finished {
  margin: 0;
  animation: ac-section-finished-in 700ms cubic-bezier(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1) 200ms both;
  color: var(--sec-ink);
  font-family: Fraunces, ui-serif, Georgia, serif;
  font-size: 22px;
  font-optical-sizing: none;
  font-variation-settings: "opsz" 14;
  text-align: center;
}

.ac-section-finished span {
  display: block;
  margin-top: 0.75rem;
  color: #64748b;
  font-family: var(--sec-sans);
  font-size: 0.875rem;
  line-height: 1.6;
}

@keyframes ac-section-finished-in {
  from {
    opacity: 0;
    transform: translateY(6px);
  }
  to {
    opacity: 1;
    transform: none;
  }
}

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  .ac-section.is-finishing,
  .ac-section.is-finishing .ac-section-head,
  .ac-section.is-finishing .ac-section-paper {
    transition: none;
  }

  .ac-section-finished {
    animation: none;
  }
}


/* The other half of the handover, on the surface that arrives.
 *
 * Applied by the Report Card's own container so the card rises onto the ground
 * the drill room just warmed to, instead of appearing on it. Slower than a
 * normal entrance and with no scale: this is the end of twenty-five minutes of
 * concentration, not a notification.
 */
.ac-section-handover {
  animation: ac-section-handover-in 800ms cubic-bezier(0.22, 1, 0.36, 1) both;
}

@keyframes ac-section-handover-in {
  from {
    opacity: 0;
    transform: translateY(10px);
  }
  to {
    opacity: 1;
    transform: none;
  }
}

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  .ac-section-handover {
    animation: none;
  }
}

/* Advancing without a keyboard.

   The drill is one key and no mouse, and on a laptop it stays exactly that:
   this control is not rendered where a keyboard exists. It appears only on a
   coarse pointer, where Space cannot be pressed at all and its absence would
   otherwise leave her reading stimulus 1 with no way to reach stimulus 2.

   It is drawn as chrome rather than as a call to action. Nothing in the footer
   may read as a score or a verdict, and a large filled button in the corner of
   a drill that judges nothing would read as both. */
.ac-section-advance {
  border: 1px solid var(--sec-rule);
  border-bottom-width: 2px;
  border-radius: 0.375rem;
  background: var(--sec-paper);
  color: var(--sec-chrome);
  padding: 0.5rem 0.875rem;
  font: inherit;
  font-size: 0.8125rem;
  /* A 25-minute run means many presses. Anything under this is a mis-tap. */
  min-height: 44px;
  cursor: pointer;
}

.ac-section-advance:disabled {
  opacity: 0.55;
  cursor: default;
}

/* ------------------------------------------------------------------ *
 * The first-run walkthrough.
 *
 * Beside the paper, in the field's own colour so it reads as chrome rather
 * than as part of the reading. It is never allowed to narrow the paper below
 * what a stimulus needs, so it collapses under the card on a small screen and
 * disappears entirely on a phone, where the loop is one column already.
 * ------------------------------------------------------------------ */
.ac-section-coach {
  flex: 0 0 19rem;
  align-self: flex-start;
  margin-left: 1rem;
  padding: 1rem 1.1rem 1.15rem;
  border: 1px solid rgb(255 255 255 / 0.18);
  border-radius: 0.5rem;
  background: rgb(255 255 255 / 0.07);
  color: rgb(255 255 255 / 0.92);
}

.ac-section-coach-step {
  margin: 0 0 0.5rem;
  font-size: 0.68rem;
  font-weight: 700;
  letter-spacing: 0.09em;
  text-transform: uppercase;
  color: rgb(255 255 255 / 0.6);
}

.ac-section-coach-title {
  margin: 0 0 0.45rem;
  font-size: 1rem;
  font-weight: 650;
  line-height: 1.3;
}

.ac-section-coach-body {
  margin: 0;
  font-size: 0.875rem;
  line-height: 1.55;
  color: rgb(255 255 255 / 0.82);
}

.ac-section-coach-next {
  margin: 0.85rem 0 0;
  padding-top: 0.7rem;
  border-top: 1px solid rgb(255 255 255 / 0.14);
  font-size: 0.82rem;
  line-height: 1.5;
  color: rgb(255 255 255 / 0.78);
}

.ac-section-coach-next span {
  margin-right: 0.35rem;
  font-weight: 700;
  letter-spacing: 0.06em;
  text-transform: uppercase;
  font-size: 0.66rem;
  color: rgb(255 255 255 / 0.55);
}

.ac-section-coach-hide {
  margin-top: 0.9rem;
  padding: 0.3rem 0.65rem;
  border: 1px solid rgb(255 255 255 / 0.25);
  border-radius: 0.3rem;
  background: transparent;
  color: rgb(255 255 255 / 0.75);
  font-size: 0.78rem;
  cursor: pointer;
}

.ac-section-coach-hide:hover {
  background: rgb(255 255 255 / 0.1);
}

@media (max-width: 1100px) {
  .ac-section-field {
    flex-wrap: wrap;
  }

  .ac-section-coach {
    flex: 1 1 100%;
    margin: 1rem 0 0;
  }
}

@media (max-width: 640px) {
  .ac-section-coach {
    padding: 0.85rem 0.9rem 1rem;
  }
}

Tool result:
# Heuristics Scoring Guide

Score each of Nielsen's 10 Usability Heuristics on a 0–4 scale. Be honest — a 4 means genuinely excellent, not "good enough."

## Nielsen's 10 Heuristics

### 1. Visibility of System Status

Keep users informed about what's happening through timely, appropriate feedback.

**Check for**:
- Loading indicators during async operations
- Confirmation of user actions (save, submit, delete)
- Progress indicators for multi-step processes
- Current location in navigation (breadcrumbs, active states)
- Form validation feedback (inline, not just on submit)

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | No feedback — user is guessing what happened |
| 1 | Rare feedback — most actions produce no visible response |
| 2 | Partial — some states communicated, major gaps remain |
| 3 | Good — most operations give clear feedback, minor gaps |
| 4 | Excellent — every action confirms, progress is always visible |

### 2. Match Between System and Real World

Speak the user's language. Follow real-world conventions. Information appears in natural, logical order.

**Check for**:
- Familiar terminology (no unexplained jargon)
- Logical information order matching user expectations
- Recognizable icons and metaphors
- Domain-appropriate language for the target audience
- Natural reading flow (left-to-right, top-to-bottom priority)

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | Pure tech jargon, alien to users |
| 1 | Mostly confusing — requires domain expertise to navigate |
| 2 | Mixed — some plain language, some jargon leaks through |
| 3 | Mostly natural — occasional term needs context |
| 4 | Speaks the user's language fluently throughout |

### 3. User Control and Freedom

Users need a clear "emergency exit" from unwanted states without extended dialogue.

**Check for**:
- Undo/redo functionality
- Cancel buttons on forms and modals
- Clear navigation back to safety (home, previous)
- Easy way to clear filters, search, selections
- Escape from long or multi-step processes

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | Users get trapped — no way out without refreshing |
| 1 | Difficult exits — must find obscure paths to escape |
| 2 | Some exits — main flows have escape, edge cases don't |
| 3 | Good control — users can exit and undo most actions |
| 4 | Full control — undo, cancel, back, and escape everywhere |

### 4. Consistency and Standards

Users shouldn't wonder whether different words, situations, or actions mean the same thing.

**Check for**:
- Consistent terminology throughout the interface
- Same actions produce same results everywhere
- Platform conventions followed (standard UI patterns)
- Visual consistency (colors, typography, spacing, components)
- Consistent interaction patterns (same gesture = same behavior)

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | Inconsistent everywhere — feels like different products stitched together |
| 1 | Many inconsistencies — similar things look/behave differently |
| 2 | Partially consistent — main flows match, details diverge |
| 3 | Mostly consistent — occasional deviation, nothing confusing |
| 4 | Fully consistent — cohesive system, predictable behavior |

### 5. Error Prevention

Better than good error messages is a design that prevents problems in the first place.

**Check for**:
- Confirmation before destructive actions (delete, overwrite)
- Constraints preventing invalid input (date pickers, dropdowns)
- Smart defaults that reduce errors
- Clear labels that prevent misunderstanding
- Autosave and draft recovery

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | Errors easy to make — no guardrails anywhere |
| 1 | Few safeguards — some inputs validated, most aren't |
| 2 | Partial prevention — common errors caught, edge cases slip |
| 3 | Good prevention — most error paths blocked proactively |
| 4 | Excellent — errors nearly impossible through smart constraints |

### 6. Recognition Rather Than Recall

Minimize memory load. Make objects, actions, and options visible or easily retrievable.

**Check for**:
- Visible options (not buried in hidden menus)
- Contextual help when needed (tooltips, inline hints)
- Recent items and history
- Autocomplete and suggestions
- Labels on icons (not icon-only navigation)

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | Heavy memorization — users must remember paths and commands |
| 1 | Mostly recall — many hidden features, few visible cues |
| 2 | Some aids — main actions visible, secondary features hidden |
| 3 | Good recognition — most things discoverable, few memory demands |
| 4 | Everything discoverable — users never need to memorize |

### 7. Flexibility and Efficiency of Use

Accelerators — invisible to novices — speed up expert interaction.

**Check for**:
- Keyboard shortcuts for common actions
- Customizable interface elements
- Recent items and favorites
- Bulk/batch actions
- Power user features that don't complicate the basics

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | One rigid path — no shortcuts or alternatives |
| 1 | Limited flexibility — few alternatives to the main path |
| 2 | Some shortcuts — basic keyboard support, limited bulk actions |
| 3 | Good accelerators — keyboard nav, some customization |
| 4 | Highly flexible — multiple paths, power features, customizable |

### 8. Aesthetic and Minimalist Design

Interfaces should not contain irrelevant or rarely needed information. Every element should serve a purpose.

**Check for**:
- Only necessary information visible at each step
- Clear visual hierarchy directing attention
- Purposeful use of color and emphasis
- No decorative clutter competing for attention
- Focused, uncluttered layouts

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | Overwhelming — everything competes for attention equally |
| 1 | Cluttered — too much noise, hard to find what matters |
| 2 | Some clutter — main content clear, periphery noisy |
| 3 | Mostly clean — focused design, minor visual noise |
| 4 | Perfectly minimal — every element earns its pixel |

### 9. Help Users Recognize, Diagnose, and Recover from Errors

Error messages should use plain language, precisely indicate the problem, and constructively suggest a solution.

**Check for**:
- Plain language error messages (no error codes for users)
- Specific problem identification ("Email is missing @" not "Invalid input")
- Actionable recovery suggestions
- Errors displayed near the source of the problem
- Non-blocking error handling (don't wipe the form)

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | Cryptic errors — codes, jargon, or no message at all |
| 1 | Vague errors — "Something went wrong" with no guidance |
| 2 | Clear but unhelpful — names the problem but not the fix |
| 3 | Clear with suggestions — identifies problem and offers next steps |
| 4 | Perfect recovery — pinpoints issue, suggests fix, preserves user work |

### 10. Help and Documentation

Even if the system is usable without docs, help should be easy to find, task-focused, and concise.

**Check for**:
- Searchable help or documentation
- Contextual help (tooltips, inline hints, guided tours)
- Task-focused organization (not feature-organized)
- Concise, scannable content
- Easy access without leaving current context

**Scoring**:
| Score | Criteria |
|-------|----------|
| 0 | No help available anywhere |
| 1 | Help exists but hard to find or irrelevant |
| 2 | Basic help — FAQ or docs exist, not contextual |
| 3 | Good documentation — searchable, mostly task-focused |
| 4 | Excellent contextual help — right info at the right moment |

---

## Score Summary

**Total possible**: 40 points (10 heuristics × 4 max)

| Score Range | Rating | What It Means |
|-------------|--------|---------------|
| 36–40 | Excellent | Minor polish only — ship it |
| 28–35 | Good | Address weak areas, solid foundation |
| 20–27 | Acceptable | Significant improvements needed before users are happy |
| 12–19 | Poor | Major UX overhaul required — core experience broken |
| 0–11 | Critical | Redesign needed — unusable in current state |

---

## Issue Severity (P0–P3)

Tag each individual issue found during scoring with a priority level:

| Priority | Name | Description | Action |
|----------|------|-------------|--------|
| **P0** | Blocking | Prevents task completion entirely | Fix immediately — this is a showstopper |
| **P1** | Major | Causes significant difficulty or confusion | Fix before release |
| **P2** | Minor | Annoyance, but workaround exists | Fix in next pass |
| **P3** | Polish | Nice-to-fix, no real user impact | Fix if time permits |

**Tip**: If you're unsure between two levels, ask: "Would a user contact support about this?" If yes, it's at least P1.
=====PERSONAS=====
# Persona-Based Design Testing

Test the interface through the eyes of 5 distinct user archetypes. Each persona exposes different failure modes that a single "design director" perspective would miss.

**How to use**: Select 2–3 personas most relevant to the interface being critiqued. Walk through the primary user action as each persona. Report specific red flags — not generic concerns.

---

## 1. Impatient Power User — "Alex"

**Profile**: Expert with similar products. Expects efficiency, hates hand-holding. Will find shortcuts or leave.

**Behaviors**:
- Skips all onboarding and instructions
- Looks for keyboard shortcuts immediately
- Tries to bulk-select, batch-edit, and automate
- Gets frustrated by required steps that feel unnecessary
- Abandons if anything feels slow or patronizing

**Test Questions**:
- Can Alex complete the core task in under 60 seconds?
- Are there keyboard shortcuts for common actions?
- Can onboarding be skipped entirely?
- Do modals have keyboard dismiss (Esc)?
- Is there a "power user" path (shortcuts, bulk actions)?

**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
- Forced tutorials or unskippable onboarding
- No keyboard navigation for primary actions
- Slow animations that can't be skipped
- One-item-at-a-time workflows where batch would be natural
- Redundant confirmation steps for low-risk actions

---

## 2. Confused First-Timer — "Jordan"

**Profile**: Never used this type of product. Needs guidance at every step. Will abandon rather than figure it out.

**Behaviors**:
- Reads all instructions carefully
- Hesitates before clicking anything unfamiliar
- Looks for help or support constantly
- Misunderstands jargon and abbreviations
- Takes the most literal interpretation of any label

**Test Questions**:
- Is the first action obviously clear within 5 seconds?
- Are all icons labeled with text?
- Is there contextual help at decision points?
- Does terminology assume prior knowledge?
- Is there a clear "back" or "undo" at every step?

**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
- Icon-only navigation with no labels
- Technical jargon without explanation
- No visible help option or guidance
- Ambiguous next steps after completing an action
- No confirmation that an action succeeded

---

## 3. Accessibility-Dependent User — "Sam"

**Profile**: Uses screen reader (VoiceOver/NVDA), keyboard-only navigation. May have low vision, motor impairment, or cognitive differences.

**Behaviors**:
- Tabs through the interface linearly
- Relies on ARIA labels and heading structure
- Cannot see hover states or visual-only indicators
- Needs adequate color contrast (4.5:1 minimum)
- May use browser zoom up to 200%

**Test Questions**:
- Can the entire primary flow be completed keyboard-only?
- Are all interactive elements focusable with visible focus indicators?
- Do images have meaningful alt text?
- Is color contrast WCAG AA compliant (4.5:1 for text)?
- Does the screen reader announce state changes (loading, success, errors)?

**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
- Click-only interactions with no keyboard alternative
- Missing or invisible focus indicators
- Meaning conveyed by color alone (red = error, green = success)
- Unlabeled form fields or buttons
- Time-limited actions without extension option
- Custom components that break screen reader flow

---

## 4. Deliberate Stress Tester — "Riley"

**Profile**: Methodical user who pushes interfaces beyond the happy path. Tests edge cases, tries unexpected inputs, and probes for gaps in the experience.

**Behaviors**:
- Tests edge cases intentionally (empty states, long strings, special characters)
- Submits forms with unexpected data (emoji, RTL text, very long values)
- Tries to break workflows by navigating backwards, refreshing mid-flow, or opening in multiple tabs
- Looks for inconsistencies between what the UI promises and what actually happens
- Documents problems methodically

**Test Questions**:
- What happens at the edges (0 items, 1000 items, very long text)?
- Do error states recover gracefully or leave the UI in a broken state?
- What happens on refresh mid-workflow? Is state preserved?
- Are there features that appear to work but produce broken results?
- How does the UI handle unexpected input (emoji, special chars, paste from Excel)?

**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
- Features that appear to work but silently fail or produce wrong results
- Error handling that exposes technical details or leaves UI in a broken state
- Empty states that show nothing useful ("No results" with no guidance)
- Workflows that lose user data on refresh or navigation
- Inconsistent behavior between similar interactions in different parts of the UI

---

## 5. Distracted Mobile User — "Casey"

**Profile**: Using phone one-handed on the go. Frequently interrupted. Possibly on a slow connection.

**Behaviors**:
- Uses thumb only — prefers bottom-of-screen actions
- Gets interrupted mid-flow and returns later
- Switches between apps frequently
- Has limited attention span and low patience
- Types as little as possible, prefers taps and selections

**Test Questions**:
- Are primary actions in the thumb zone (bottom half of screen)?
- Is state preserved if the user leaves and returns?
- Does it work on slow connections (3G)?
- Can forms leverage autocomplete and smart defaults?
- Are touch targets at least 44×44pt?

**Red Flags** (report these specifically):
- Important actions positioned at the top of the screen (unreachable by thumb)
- No state persistence — progress lost on tab switch or interruption
- Large text inputs required where selection would work
- Heavy assets loading on every page (no lazy loading)
- Tiny tap targets or targets too close together

---

## Selecting Personas

Choose personas based on the interface type:

| Interface Type | Primary Personas | Why |
|---------------|-----------------|-----|
| Landing page / marketing | Jordan, Riley, Casey | First impressions, trust, mobile |
| Dashboard / admin | Alex, Sam | Power users, accessibility |
| E-commerce / checkout | Casey, Riley, Jordan | Mobile, edge cases, clarity |
| Onboarding flow | Jordan, Casey | Confusion, interruption |
| Data-heavy / analytics | Alex, Sam | Efficiency, keyboard nav |
| Form-heavy / wizard | Jordan, Sam, Casey | Clarity, accessibility, mobile |

---

## Project-Specific Personas

If `AGENTS.md` contains a `## Design Context` section (generated by `impeccable teach`), derive 1–2 additional personas from the audience and brand information:

1. Read the target audience description
2. Identify the primary user archetype not covered by the 5 predefined personas
3. Create a persona following this template:

```
### [Role] — "[Name]"

**Profile**: [2-3 key characteristics derived from Design Context]

**Behaviors**: [3-4 specific behaviors based on the described audience]

**Red Flags**: [3-4 things that would alienate this specific user type]
```

Only generate project-specific personas when real Design Context data is available. Don't invent audience details — use the 5 predefined personas when no context exists.
=====COGLOAD=====
# Cognitive Load Assessment

Cognitive load is the total mental effort required to use an interface. Overloaded users make mistakes, get frustrated, and leave. This reference helps identify and fix cognitive overload.

---

## Three Types of Cognitive Load

### Intrinsic Load — The Task Itself
Complexity inherent to what the user is trying to do. You can't eliminate this, but you can structure it.

**Manage it by**:
- Breaking complex tasks into discrete steps
- Providing scaffolding (templates, defaults, examples)
- Progressive disclosure — show what's needed now, hide the rest
- Grouping related decisions together

### Extraneous Load — Bad Design
Mental effort caused by poor design choices. **Eliminate this ruthlessly** — it's pure waste.

**Common sources**:
- Confusing navigation that requires mental mapping
- Unclear labels that force users to guess meaning
- Visual clutter competing for attention
- Inconsistent patterns that prevent learning
- Unnecessary steps between user intent and result

### Germane Load — Learning Effort
Mental effort spent building understanding. This is *good* cognitive load — it leads to mastery.

**Support it by**:
- Progressive disclosure that reveals complexity gradually
- Consistent patterns that reward learning
- Feedback that confirms correct understanding
- Onboarding that teaches through action, not walls of text

---

## Cognitive Load Checklist

Evaluate the interface against these 8 items:

- [ ] **Single focus**: Can the user complete their primary task without distraction from competing elements?
- [ ] **Chunking**: Is information presented in digestible groups (≤4 items per group)?
- [ ] **Grouping**: Are related items visually grouped together (proximity, borders, shared background)?
- [ ] **Visual hierarchy**: Is it immediately clear what's most important on the screen?
- [ ] **One thing at a time**: Can the user focus on a single decision before moving to the next?
- [ ] **Minimal choices**: Are decisions simplified (≤4 visible options at any decision point)?
- [ ] **Working memory**: Does the user need to remember information from a previous screen to act on the current one?
- [ ] **Progressive disclosure**: Is complexity revealed only when the user needs it?

**Scoring**: Count the failed items. 0–1 failures = low cognitive load (good). 2–3 = moderate (address soon). 4+ = high cognitive load (critical fix needed).

---

## The Working Memory Rule

**Humans can hold ≤4 items in working memory at once** (Miller's Law revised by Cowan, 2001).

At any decision point, count the number of distinct options, actions, or pieces of information a user must simultaneously consider:
- **≤4 items**: Within working memory limits — manageable
- **5–7 items**: Pushing the boundary — consider grouping or progressive disclosure
- **8+ items**: Overloaded — users will skip, misclick, or abandon

**Practical applications**:
- Navigation menus: ≤5 top-level items (group the rest under clear categories)
- Form sections: ≤4 fields visible per group before a visual break
- Action buttons: 1 primary, 1–2 secondary, group the rest in a menu
- Dashboard widgets: ≤4 key metrics visible without scrolling
- Pricing tiers: ≤3 options (more causes analysis paralysis)

---

## Common Cognitive Load Violations

### 1. The Wall of Options
**Problem**: Presenting 10+ choices at once with no hierarchy.
**Fix**: Group into categories, highlight recommended, use progressive disclosure.

### 2. The Memory Bridge
**Problem**: User must remember info from step 1 to complete step 3.
**Fix**: Keep relevant context visible, or repeat it where it's needed.

### 3. The Hidden Navigation
**Problem**: User must build a mental map of where things are.
**Fix**: Always show current location (breadcrumbs, active states, progress indicators).

### 4. The Jargon Barrier
**Problem**: Technical or domain language forces translation effort.
**Fix**: Use plain language. If domain terms are unavoidable, define them inline.

### 5. The Visual Noise Floor
**Problem**: Every element has the same visual weight — nothing stands out.
**Fix**: Establish clear hierarchy: one primary element, 2–3 secondary, everything else muted.

### 6. The Inconsistent Pattern
**Problem**: Similar actions work differently in different places.
**Fix**: Standardize interaction patterns. Same type of action = same type of UI.

### 7. The Multi-Task Demand
**Problem**: Interface requires processing multiple simultaneous inputs (reading + deciding + navigating).
**Fix**: Sequence the steps. Let the user do one thing at a time.

### 8. The Context Switch
**Problem**: User must jump between screens/tabs/modals to gather info for a single decision.
**Fix**: Co-locate the information needed for each decision. Reduce back-and-forth.
=====PRODUCT REF=====
# Product register

When design SERVES the product: app UIs, admin dashboards, settings panels, data tables, tools, authenticated surfaces, anything where the user is in a task.

## The product slop test

Not "would someone say AI made this" — familiarity is often a feature here. The test is: would a user fluent in the category's best tools (Linear, Figma, Notion, Raycast, Stripe come to mind) sit down and trust this interface, or pause at every subtly-off component?

Product UI's failure mode isn't flatness, it's strangeness without purpose: over-decorated buttons, mismatched form controls, gratuitous motion, display fonts where labels should be, invented affordances for standard tasks. The bar is earned familiarity. The tool should disappear into the task.

## Typography

- **System fonts are legitimate.** `-apple-system, BlinkMacSystemFont, "Segoe UI", system-ui, sans-serif` gives you native feel on every platform. Inter is the common cross-platform default for a reason.
- **One family is often right.** Product UIs don't need display/body pairing. A well-tuned sans carries headings, buttons, labels, body, data.
- **Fixed rem scale, not fluid.** Clamp-sized headings don't serve product UI. Users view at consistent DPI, and a fluid h1 that shrinks in a sidebar looks worse, not better.
- **Tighter scale ratio.** 1.125–1.2 between steps is typical. More type elements here than on brand surfaces; exaggerated contrast creates noise.
- **Line length still applies for prose** (65–75ch). Data and compact UI can run denser — tables at 120ch+ are fine.

## Color

Product defaults to Restrained. A single surface can earn Committed — a dashboard where one category color carries a report, an onboarding flow with a drenched welcome screen — but Restrained is the floor.

- State-rich semantic vocabulary: hover, focus, active, disabled, selected, loading, error, warning, success, info. Standardize these.
- Accent color used for primary actions, current selection, and state indicators only — not decoration.
- A second neutral layer for sidebars, toolbars, and panels (slightly cooler or warmer than the content surface).

## Layout

- Predictable grids. Consistency IS an affordance — users navigate faster when the structure is expected.
- Familiar patterns are features. Standard navigation (top bar, side nav), breadcrumbs, tabs, and form layouts have established user expectations. Don't reinvent for flavor.
- Responsive behavior is structural (collapse sidebar, responsive table, breakpoint-driven columns), not fluid typography.

## Components

Every interactive component has: default, hover, focus, active, disabled, loading, error. Don't ship with half of these.

- Skeleton states for loading, not spinners in the middle of content.
- Empty states that teach the interface, not "nothing here."
- Consistent affordances across the surface. Same button shape. Same form-control vocabulary. Same icon style.

## Motion

- 150–250 ms on most transitions. Users are in flow — don't make them wait for choreography.
- Motion conveys state, not decoration. State change, feedback, loading, reveal — nothing else.
- No orchestrated page-load sequences. Product loads into a task; users don't want to watch it load.

## Product bans (on top of the shared absolute bans)

- Decorative motion that doesn't convey state.
- Inconsistent component vocabulary across screens. If the "save" button looks different in two places, one is wrong.
- Display fonts in UI labels, buttons, data.
- Reinventing standard affordances for flavor (custom scrollbars, weird form controls, non-standard modals).
- Heavy color or full-saturation accents on inactive states.

## Product permissions

Product can afford things brand surfaces can't.

- System fonts and familiar sans defaults (Inter, SF Pro, system-ui stacks).
- Standard navigation patterns: top bar + side nav, breadcrumbs, tabs, command palettes.
- Density. Tables with many rows, panels with many labels, dense information when users need it.
- Consistency over surprise. The same visual vocabulary screen to screen is a virtue; delight is saved for moments, not pages.
